<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q001"></a>
## Q1. What will follow if a Money Bill is substantially amended by the Rajya Sabha?
**Primary route:** Polity → [Parliament](../../topics/polity/parliament.md) → What will follow if a Money Bill is substantially amended by the Rajya Sabha?
**Primary classification:** Polity → Parliament → What will follow if a Money Bill is substantially amended by the Rajya Sabha?
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q1 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Parliament.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Parliament.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-what-will-follow-if-a-money-bill-is-substantially-amended-by-the-rajya-sabha`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

What will follow if a Money Bill is substantially amended by the Rajya Sabha?

(a) The Lok Sabha may still proceed with the Bill, accepting or not accepting the recommendations of the Rajya Sabha  
(b) The Lok Sabha cannot consider the Bill further  
(c) The Lok Sabha may send the Bill to the Rajya Sabha for reconsideration  
(d) The President may call a joint sitting for passing the Bill  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — The Lok Sabha may still proceed with the Bill, accepting or not accepting the recommendations of the Rajya Sabha**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

According to Article 109 of the Constitution of India, a Money Bill can only be introduced in the Lok Sabha. After it is passed by the Lok Sabha, it is transmitted to the Rajya Sabha for its recommendations. The Rajya Sabha must return the bill within a period of 14 days. The Lok Sabha has the absolute power to either accept or reject any or all of the recommendations made by the Rajya Sabha (Article 109(2)). If the Lok Sabha accepts any of the recommendations, the bill is deemed to have been passed by both Houses with those amendments. If the Lok Sabha does not accept any recommendations, the bill is deemed to have been passed by both Houses in the form in which it was originally passed by the Lok Sabha (Article 109(3)). Furthermore, Article 108, which provides for a joint sitting of both Houses in case of a deadlock, does not apply to Money Bills. This ensures the supremacy of the Lok Sabha in financial matters (Source: M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 22: Parliament; Constitution of India, Article 109).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — The Lok Sabha may still proceed with the Bill, accepting or not accepting the recommendations of the Rajya Sabha. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: The Lok Sabha may still proceed with the Bill, accepting or not accepting the recommendations of the Rajya Sabha** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **B: The Lok Sabha cannot consider the Bill further** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: The Lok Sabha may send the Bill to the Rajya Sabha for reconsideration** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: The President may call a joint sitting for passing the Bill** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.
**Evidence anchor:** `Polity/basic/Parliament.md`; held Series-A booklet Q1; held official-final Series-A key grid.

<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q002"></a>
## Q2. Which one of the following statements is correct?
**Primary route:** Polity → [Governor and Chief Minister](../../topics/polity/governor-and-cm.md) → Which one of the following statements is correct?
**Primary classification:** Polity → Governor and Chief Minister → Which one of the following statements is correct?
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q2 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Governor-and-CM.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Governor-and-CM.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-which-one-of-the-following-statements-is-correct`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which one of the following statements is correct?

(a) In India, the same person cannot be appointed as Governor for two or more States at the same time  
(b) The Judges of the High Court of the States in India are appointed by the Governor of the State just as the Judges of the Supreme Court are appointed by the President  
(c) No procedure has been laid down in the Constitution of India for the removal of a Governor from his/her post  
(d) In the case of a Union Territory having a legislative setup, the Chief Minister is appointed by the Lt. Governor on the basis of majority support  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — No procedure has been laid down in the Constitution of India for the removal of a Governor from his/her post**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Option 2 is correct because the Constitution of India does not prescribe a specific procedure or grounds for the removal of a Governor. According to Article 156(1), the Governor holds office during the 'pleasure of the President'. Unlike the President (who can be impeached under Article 61) or Judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts (who can be removed by an order of the President after an address by Parliament under Articles 124(4) and 217), the Governor can be removed by the President at any time without a formal process. 

Regarding the other options:
- Option 0 is incorrect: The 7th Constitutional Amendment Act of 1956 amended Article 153 to allow the appointment of the same person as Governor for two or more States.
- Option 1 is incorrect: Under Article 217, Judges of the High Court are appointed by the President of India, not the Governor. The Governor is only consulted during the process.
- Option 3 is incorrect: In Union Territories with a legislative assembly (like Delhi and Puducherry), the Chief Minister is appointed by the President, not the Lt. Governor. For Delhi, this is specified under Article 239AA(5) of the Constitution, and for Puducherry, under Section 45 of the Government of Union Territories Act, 1963.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — No procedure has been laid down in the Constitution of India for the removal of a Governor from his/her post. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: In India, the same person cannot be appointed as Governor for two or more States at the same time** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: The Judges of the High Court of the States in India are appointed by the Governor of the State just as the Judges of the Supreme Court are appointed by the President** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: No procedure has been laid down in the Constitution of India for the removal of a Governor from his/her post** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: In the case of a Union Territory having a legislative setup, the Chief Minister is appointed by the Lt. Governor on the basis of majority support** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

## Q3. Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched? Geographical Feature : Region
**Primary route:** Geography → [World Regional Geography: Continents and Countries](../../topics/geography/world-regional-geography-continents-countries.md) → Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched? Geographical Feature : Region
**Primary classification:** Geography → World Regional Geography: Continents and Countries → Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched? Geographical Feature : Region
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q3 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Geography/basic/34_World-Regional-Geography-Continents-Countries.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Geography/basic/34_World-Regional-Geography-Continents-Countries.md)
**Canonical slug:** `geography-which-one-of-the-following-pairs-is-correctly-matched-geographical-feature-region`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched?
**Geographical Feature : Region**

(a) Abyssinian Plateau : Arabia  
(b) Atlas Mountains : North-Western Africa  
(c) Guiana Highlands : South-Western Africa  
(d) Okavango Basin : Patagonia  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — Atlas Mountains : North-Western Africa**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The correct pair is Atlas Mountains : North-Western Africa. 

1. **Abyssinian Plateau:** Also known as the Ethiopian Highlands, this rugged mass of mountains is located in Ethiopia and Eritrea in **East Africa**, not Arabia (Britannica, 'Ethiopian Plateau'). 
2. **Atlas Mountains:** This mountain system is located in **North-Western Africa**, stretching approximately 2,500 km through Morocco, Algeria, and Tunisia. It separates the Mediterranean and Atlantic coastlines from the Sahara Desert (Britannica, 'Atlas Mountains'). 
3. **Guiana Highlands:** This plateau and low-mountain region is located in **Northern South America**, spanning Venezuela, Guyana, Suriname, French Guiana, northern Brazil, and southeastern Colombia. It is not located in South-Western Africa (Britannica, 'Guiana Highlands'). 
4. **Okavango Basin:** The Okavango Basin is an endorheic basin located in **Southern Africa**, primarily covering parts of Angola, Namibia, and Botswana. **Patagonia**, on the other hand, is a geographical region located at the southern end of **South America**, shared by Argentina and Chile (National Geographic, 'Okavango Delta'; Britannica, 'Patagonia').

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — Atlas Mountains : North-Western Africa. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: Abyssinian Plateau : Arabia** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **B: Atlas Mountains : North-Western Africa** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **C: Guiana Highlands : South-Western Africa** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: Okavango Basin : Patagonia** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option B. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

**Primary classification:** Art and Culture → Mauryan, Buddhist, Jain and Rock-Cut Heritage → With reference to the history of Indian rock-cut architecture, consider the following statements: 1. T...

**Canonical owner:** [Indian-Art-and-Culture/basic/02_Mauryan-Buddhist-Jain-and-Rock-Cut-Heritage.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Indian-Art-and-Culture/basic/02_Mauryan-Buddhist-Jain-and-Rock-Cut-Heritage.md)
**Canonical slug:** `art-and-culture-with-reference-to-the-history-of-indian-rock-cut-architecture-consider-the-following-statements-1-the-caves-at-badami-ar`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

With reference to the history of Indian rock-cut architecture, consider the following statements:
1. The caves at Badami are the oldest surviving rock-cut caves in India.
2. The Barabar rock-cut caves were originally made for Ajivikas by Emperor Chandragupta Maurya.
3. At Ellora, caves were made for different faiths.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Statement 1 is incorrect: The oldest surviving rock-cut caves in India are the Barabar Caves in Bihar, which date back to the Mauryan period (3rd century BCE). The Badami caves in Karnataka were constructed much later, during the 6th century CE by the Chalukya dynasty (Source: NCERT Class 11, An Introduction to Indian Art, Chapter 3). Statement 2 is incorrect: While the Barabar caves were indeed made for the Ajivika sect, they were commissioned by the Mauryan Emperor Ashoka and his grandson Dasharatha, not Chandragupta Maurya (Source: NCERT Class 11, An Introduction to Indian Art, Chapter 3). Statement 3 is correct: The Ellora caves (Maharashtra) are a unique example of religious harmony, containing 34 monasteries and temples belonging to three different faiths: Buddhism (Caves 1–12), Hinduism (Caves 13–29), and Jainism (Caves 30–34), constructed between the 5th and 10th centuries CE (Source: NCERT Class 11, An Introduction to Indian Art, Chapter 5).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — 3 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: 3 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

**Primary route:** Science and Technology → [Genetic Engineering, GM Crops and CRISPR](../../topics/science-and-technology/genetic-engineering-gm-crops-and-crispr.md) → Recombinant DNA technology (Genetic Engineering) allows genes to be transferred 1. across different sp...
**Primary classification:** Science and Technology → Genetic Engineering, GM Crops and CRISPR → Recombinant DNA technology (Genetic Engineering) allows genes to be transferred 1. across different sp...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q5 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Science-and-Technology/basic/14_Genetic-Engineering-GM-Crops-and-CRISPR.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Science-and-Technology/basic/14_Genetic-Engineering-GM-Crops-and-CRISPR.md)
**Canonical slug:** `science-and-technology-recombinant-dna-technology-genetic-engineering-allows-genes-to-be-transferred-1-across-different-species-of-plants-2-fro`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Recombinant DNA technology (Genetic Engineering) allows genes to be transferred
1. across different species of plants
2. from animals to plants
3. from microorganisms to higher organisms
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 1 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — 1, 2 and 3**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Recombinant DNA technology, also known as genetic engineering, involves the manipulation of DNA to create new genetic combinations by joining DNA molecules from different sources. This technology is not restricted by natural biological barriers or phylogenetic distances, allowing for the transfer of genes across diverse kingdoms of life. 

1. **Across different species of plants:** Genetic engineering is widely used in agriculture to transfer desirable traits, such as herbicide tolerance or drought resistance, between different plant species. For example, genes from one plant species can be inserted into another to improve crop yield or resilience (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 12).

2. **From animals to plants:** It is scientifically possible to transfer genes from animals to plants. A well-known experimental example is the transfer of an 'antifreeze' gene from the Arctic Flounder (a fish) into plants like tomatoes and strawberries to enhance their frost resistance (Source: Britannica, Recombinant DNA).

3. **From microorganisms to higher organisms:** This is one of the most common applications of the technology. A prominent example is **Bt Cotton**, where genes from the soil bacterium *Bacillus thuringiensis* are inserted into the cotton plant (a higher organism) to provide resistance against pests like bollworms. Similarly, the production of recombinant human insulin involves inserting human genes into microorganisms like *E. coli* or yeast, demonstrating the fluid exchange of genetic material between simple and complex organisms (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 11).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — 1, 2 and 3. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: 1 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

**Primary classification:** Ancient History → Post-Gupta, Harsha and Eastern India → The Chinese traveller Yuan Chwang (Hiuen Tsang) who visited India recorded the general conditions and...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q6 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Ancient-Indian-History/basic/22_Post-Gupta-Harsha-and-Eastern-India.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Ancient-Indian-History/basic/22_Post-Gupta-Harsha-and-Eastern-India.md)

**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The Chinese traveller Yuan Chwang (Hiuen Tsang) who visited India recorded the general conditions and culture of India at that time. In this context, which of the following statements is/are correct?
1. The roads and river-routes were completely immune from robbery.
2. As regards punishment for offences, ordeals by fire, water and poison were the instruments for determining the innocence or guilt of a person.
3. The tradesmen had to pay duties at ferries and barrier stations.
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 1 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — 2 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Based on the accounts of the Chinese traveler Yuan Chwang (Hiuen Tsang), who visited India during the reign of King Harshavardhana (606–647 AD), the following observations were made:

1. **Statement 1 is incorrect**: While Harsha's administration was generally efficient, the roads and river routes were not completely safe. Yuan Chwang himself recorded that he was robbed multiple times during his travels, including an incident where he was nearly sacrificed by pirates on the Ganges river (Source: 'Si-Yu-Ki' by Hiuen Tsang; NCERT Class 12 History).

2. **Statement 2 is correct**: Yuan Chwang described the judicial system as being quite severe. He specifically mentioned that for determining the innocence or guilt of an accused person, four types of ordeals were used: ordeal by water, by fire, by weighing, and by poison (Source: 'On Yuan Chwang's Travels in India' by Thomas Watters; Tamil Nadu State Board Class 11 History, Chapter 8).

3. **Statement 3 is correct**: Regarding the economy and taxation, he noted that the government was liberal and taxation was light. However, he explicitly mentioned that tradesmen and merchants had to pay light duties at ferries and barrier stations when transporting goods (Source: 'The Life of Hiuen-Tsiang' by Shaman Hwui Li; Rau's IAS Compass).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — 2 and 3 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **B: 2 and 3 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **C: 1 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option B. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following:
1. Star tortoise
2. Monitor lizard
3. Pygmy hog
4. Spider monkey
Which of the above are naturally found in India?

(a) 1, 2 and 3 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 1 and 4 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — 1, 2 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Based on biological distribution and geographical records, the following animals are naturally found in India:
1. **Star Tortoise (Geochelone elegans)**: This species is native to the Indian subcontinent, specifically found in the dry scrub forests and grasslands of western, central, and southern India, as well as parts of Pakistan and Sri Lanka (canonical topic evidence, theTurtleRoom).
2. **Monitor Lizard**: India is home to four distinct species of monitor lizards: the Bengal monitor (*Varanus bengalensis*), the Asian water monitor (*Varanus salvator*), the Yellow monitor (*Varanus flavescens*), and the Desert monitor (*Varanus griseus*). These are widely distributed across various habitats in the country (Roundglass Sustain, canonical topic evidence).
3. **Pygmy Hog (Porcula salvania)**: This is the world's smallest wild pig and is endemic to India. Its natural habitat is restricted to the tall, wet grasslands of the southern Himalayan foothills, with the only known viable wild population residing in Manas National Park, Assam (Durrell Wildlife Conservation Trust, Animal Diversity Web).
4. **Spider Monkey (Ateles)**: Spider monkeys are 'New World monkeys' native to the tropical rainforests of Central and South America, ranging from southern Mexico to Brazil. They are not naturally found in India or any part of the Old World (Britannica, canonical topic evidence).

Therefore, only 1, 2, and 3 are naturally found in India, making Option 0 the correct choice.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — 1, 2 and 3 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1, 2 and 3 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **B: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: 1 and 4 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: 1, 2, 3 and 4** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

**Primary classification:** Environment → Water Pollution and River-Cleaning Missions → Which of the following can be found as pollutants in the drinking water in some parts of India? 1. Ars...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q8 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/14_Water-Pollution-and-River-Cleaning-Missions.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/14_Water-Pollution-and-River-Cleaning-Missions.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-which-of-the-following-can-be-found-as-pollutants-in-the-drinking-water-in-some-parts-of-india-1-arsenic-2-sorbitol-3-fl`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which of the following can be found as pollutants in the drinking water in some parts of India?
1. Arsenic
2. Sorbitol
3. Fluoride
4. Formaldehyde
5. Uranium
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 and 3 only  
(b) 2, 4 and 5 only  
(c) 1, 3 and 5 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 1, 3 and 5 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

In India, the primary pollutants found in drinking water (specifically groundwater) in various regions are Arsenic, Fluoride, and Uranium. 

1. **Arsenic (1):** Arsenic contamination is a severe geogenic (natural) issue in the Ganga-Brahmaputra fluvial plains, affecting states like West Bengal, Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, and Assam. Chronic exposure leads to health issues such as skin lesions and 'Blackfoot disease' (Source: CGWB, Ministry of Jal Shakti).
2. **Fluoride (3):** Fluoride is one of the most widespread pollutants in India, affecting over 20 states including Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, and Telangana. Excessive intake causes dental and skeletal fluorosis (Source: National Programme for Prevention and Control of Fluorosis).
3. **Uranium (5):** Uranium has been increasingly detected in groundwater above the WHO limit (30 µg/L) in states like Punjab, Haryana, Rajasthan, and parts of Andhra Pradesh. A 2020 PIB release confirmed localized occurrences of Uranium in 16 states (Source: PIB, March 2020).

**Sorbitol (2)** is a sugar alcohol used as a sweetener and is not a recognized drinking water pollutant. **Formaldehyde (4)** is an industrial chemical; while it can be a localized pollutant near specific industrial sites, it is not a widespread or characteristic pollutant of drinking water in 'some parts of India' in the same context as the others. Therefore, the correct combination is 1, 3, and 5.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — 1, 3 and 5 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: 2, 4 and 5 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: 1, 3 and 5 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q9. With reference to Indian History, the Members of the Constituent Assembly from the Provinces were
**Primary route:** Polity → [Making of the Constitution](../../topics/polity/making-of-the-constitution.md) → With reference to Indian History, the Members of the Constituent Assembly from the Provinces were
**Primary classification:** Polity → Making of the Constitution → With reference to Indian History, the Members of the Constituent Assembly from the Provinces were
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q9 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Making-of-the-Constitution.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Making-of-the-Constitution.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-with-reference-to-indian-history-the-members-of-the-constituent-assembly-from-the-provinces-were`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

With reference to Indian History, the Members of the Constituent Assembly from the Provinces were

(a) directly elected by the people of those Provinces  
(b) nominated by the Indian National Congress and the Muslim League  
(c) elected by the Provincial Legislative Assemblies  
(d) selected by the Government for their expertise in constitutional matters  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — elected by the Provincial Legislative Assemblies**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The members of the Constituent Assembly from the British Indian Provinces were elected by the members of the Provincial Legislative Assemblies. This process was based on the recommendations of the Cabinet Mission Plan of 1946. According to the plan, the total strength of the Assembly was 389, with 296 seats allotted to British India and 93 to the Princely States. The seats in each province were distributed among three main communities—Muslims, Sikhs, and General—in proportion to their population. The representatives of each community in the Provincial Legislative Assembly elected their own representatives using the method of proportional representation by means of a single transferable vote. This was an indirect election because the members of the Provincial Legislative Assemblies themselves had been elected on a limited franchise (based on property, tax, or education) under the Government of India Act, 1935, rather than universal adult franchise. In contrast, the representatives of the Princely States were nominated by the heads of those states. Thus, the Constituent Assembly was a partly elected and partly nominated body (NCERT Class 11, Indian Constitution at Work, Chapter 1; M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 2).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — elected by the Provincial Legislative Assemblies. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: directly elected by the people of those Provinces** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: nominated by the Indian National Congress and the Muslim League** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: elected by the Provincial Legislative Assemblies** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: selected by the Government for their expertise in constitutional matters** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

**Primary classification:** Environment → Coastal and Marine Ecology, CRZ and Blue Economy → Consider the following animals: 1. Sea cow 2. Sea horse 3. Sea lion Which of the above is/are mammal/m...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q10 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-consider-the-following-animals-1-sea-cow-2-sea-horse-3-sea-lion-which-of-the-above-is-are-mammal-mammals`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following animals:
1. Sea cow
2. Sea horse
3. Sea lion
Which of the above is/are mammal/mammals?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 1 and 3 only  
(c) 2 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — 1 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

1. Sea cow (Dugong): The Sea cow, specifically the Dugong, is a herbivorous marine mammal. It belongs to the order Sirenia. In India, Dugongs are found in the Gulf of Mannar, Palk Bay, Gulf of Kutch, and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands. They are listed as 'Vulnerable' on the IUCN Red List and are protected under Schedule I of the Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972 (Source: PIB, 'Conservation of Dugongs in India'; NCERT Class 11 Biology, Chapter 4). 2. Sea horse: Despite its name and horse-like head, the sea horse is a type of teleost (bony) fish belonging to the genus Hippocampus. They breathe through gills and possess a swim bladder, which are defining characteristics of fish, not mammals (Source: Britannica, 'Seahorse'). 3. Sea lion: Sea lions are marine mammals belonging to the suborder Pinnipedia. They are characterized by external ear flaps, long foreflippers, and the ability to move on land using all four flippers. Like all mammals, they are warm-blooded, breathe air, and nurse their young with milk (Source: Britannica, 'Sea lion'). Therefore, only the Sea cow and Sea lion are mammals.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — 1 and 3 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**

- **B: 1 and 3 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **C: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option B. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q11. Consider the following statements: 1. An amendment to the Constitution of India can be initiated by an...
**Primary route:** Polity → [Amendment and Basic Structure](../../topics/polity/amendment-and-basic-structure.md) → Consider the following statements: 1. An amendment to the Constitution of India can be initiated by an...
**Primary classification:** Polity → Amendment and Basic Structure → Consider the following statements: 1. An amendment to the Constitution of India can be initiated by an...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q11 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Amendment-and-Basic-Structure.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Amendment-and-Basic-Structure.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-consider-the-following-statements-1-an-amendment-to-the-constitution-of-india-can-be-initiated-by-an-introduction-of-a-b`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following statements:
1. An amendment to the Constitution of India can be initiated by an introduction of a bill in the Lok Sabha only.
2. If such an amendment seeks to make changes in the federal character of the Constitution, the amendment also requires to be ratified by the legislature of all the States of India.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 only  
(c) Both 1 and 2  
(d) Neither 1 nor 2  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — Neither 1 nor 2**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Both statements are incorrect based on the provisions of Article 368 of the Constitution of India. 

1. **Statement 1 is incorrect**: According to Article 368(2), an amendment to the Constitution can be initiated by the introduction of a Bill for the purpose in **either House of Parliament** (Lok Sabha or Rajya Sabha), not just the Lok Sabha. It cannot be initiated in the state legislatures (Constitution of India, Art. 368(2); M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter: Amendment of the Constitution).

2. **Statement 2 is incorrect**: If an amendment seeks to change the federal character of the Constitution (such as the election of the President, the extent of executive power of the Union and States, or the distribution of legislative powers), it must be ratified by the legislatures of **not less than one-half of the States**, not all the States. This ratification is done by a simple majority in the state legislatures (Constitution of India, Art. 368(2) Proviso; NCERT Class 11, Indian Constitution at Work, Chapter 9).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — Neither 1 nor 2. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: 2 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: Both 1 and 2** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: Neither 1 nor 2** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

## Q12. Consider the following statements: Attorney General of India can 1. take part in the proceedings of th...

**Primary classification:** Polity → Attorney-General → Consider the following statements: Attorney General of India can 1. take part in the proceedings of th...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q12 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Attorney-General.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Attorney-General.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-consider-the-following-statements-attorney-general-of-india-can-1-take-part-in-the-proceedings-of-the-lok-sabha-2-be-a-m`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following statements: Attorney General of India can
1. take part in the proceedings of the Lok Sabha
2. be a member of a committee of the Lok Sabha
3. speak in the Lok Sabha
4. vote in the Lok Sabha
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 4  
(c) 1, 2 and 3  
(d) 1 and 3 only  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 1, 2 and 3**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Attorney General of India (AG) is the highest law officer in the country, as established under Article 76 of the Constitution. According to Article 88 of the Constitution of India, the Attorney General has the right to speak in, and otherwise take part in the proceedings of, either House (Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha), any joint sitting of the Houses, and any committee of Parliament of which he may be named a member. This confirms that statements 1, 2, and 3 are correct. However, Article 88 explicitly states that the Attorney General 'shall not by virtue of this article be entitled to vote.' Therefore, statement 4 is incorrect. Additionally, under Article 105(4), the Attorney General enjoys all the privileges and immunities that are available to a Member of Parliament while participating in these proceedings (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter on Attorney General of India).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — 1, 2 and 3. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: 2 and 4** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: 1, 2 and 3** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: 1 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q13. With reference to the usefulness of the by-products of sugar industry, which of the following statemen...
**Primary route:** Economy → [Food Processing, Cold Chains and Value Addition](../../topics/economy/food-processing-cold-chains-and-value-addition.md) → With reference to the usefulness of the by-products of sugar industry, which of the following statemen...
**Primary classification:** Economy → Food Processing, Cold Chains and Value Addition → With reference to the usefulness of the by-products of sugar industry, which of the following statemen...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q13 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Economy/basic/15_Food-Processing-Cold-Chains-and-Value-Addition.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Economy/basic/15_Food-Processing-Cold-Chains-and-Value-Addition.md)
**Canonical slug:** `economy-with-reference-to-the-usefulness-of-the-by-products-of-sugar-industry-which-of-the-following-statements-is-are-correct-1`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

With reference to the usefulness of the by-products of sugar industry, which of the following statements is/are correct?
1. Bagasse can be used as biomass fuel for the generation of energy.
2. Molasses can be used as one of the feedstocks for the production of synthetic chemical fertilizers.
3. Molasses can be used for the production of ethanol.
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 1 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 1 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The question evaluates the industrial utility of sugar industry by-products: bagasse and molasses. 
1. **Bagasse**: This is the fibrous residue left after sugarcane is crushed to extract juice. It is a significant source of biomass fuel used in sugar mills for 'cogeneration' (producing both heat/steam and electricity). It is also used in the production of paper, pulp, and building materials (Source: canonical topic evidence, Drishti IAS). Thus, Statement 1 is correct.
2. **Molasses**: This is a thick, dark syrup left after the extraction of sugar from sugarcane juice. While molasses is used as an organic fertilizer or soil amendment (providing carbon and potassium to soil microbes), it is not a feedstock for 'synthetic chemical fertilizers' like Urea or DAP, which are typically derived from inorganic sources such as natural gas (for ammonia) and rock phosphate (Source: Edukemy, ExamRobot). Thus, Statement 2 is incorrect.
3. **Ethanol Production**: Molasses is the primary raw material for the production of ethanol through the process of fermentation and distillation. This is central to India's Ethanol Blending Program (EBP) (Source: PIB, Drishti IAS). Thus, Statement 3 is correct.
Based on the official UPSC 2013 answer key, only statements 1 and 3 are correct.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — 1 and 3 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q014"></a>
## Q14. Variations in the length of daytime and nighttime from season to season are due to
**Primary route:** Geography → [The Earth and the Universe](../../topics/geography/the-earth-and-the-universe.md) → Variations in the length of daytime and nighttime from season to season are due to
**Primary classification:** Geography → The Earth and the Universe → Variations in the length of daytime and nighttime from season to season are due to
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q14 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Geography/basic/01_The-Earth-and-the-Universe.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Geography/basic/01_The-Earth-and-the-Universe.md)
**Canonical slug:** `geography-variations-in-the-length-of-daytime-and-nighttime-from-season-to-season-are-due-to`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Variations in the length of daytime and nighttime from season to season are due to

(a) the earth's rotation on its axis  
(b) the earth's revolution round the sun in an elliptical manner  
(c) latitudinal position of the place  
(d) revolution of the earth on a tilted axis  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — revolution of the earth on a tilted axis**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The variation in the length of daytime and nighttime from season to season is primarily caused by the Earth's revolution around the Sun on a tilted axis. The Earth's axis is tilted at an angle of 23.5 degrees relative to the plane of its orbit (or 66.5 degrees with its orbital plane). As the Earth revolves around the Sun, this fixed tilt causes different parts of the Earth to receive varying amounts of sunlight at different times of the year. For instance, during the Summer Solstice (around June 21), the Northern Hemisphere is tilted towards the Sun, leading to longer days and shorter nights. Conversely, during the Winter Solstice (around December 22), the Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun, resulting in shorter days and longer nights. If the Earth's axis were not tilted, all places on Earth would experience equal lengths of day and night (12 hours each) throughout the year, regardless of the Earth's position in its orbit (NCERT Class 6 Geography, Chapter 3). While the elliptical orbit (Option 1) affects the Earth's distance from the Sun and its orbital speed, it is not the cause of seasonal day-length variation. Rotation (Option 0) causes the daily cycle of day and night, and latitudinal position (Option 2) determines the magnitude of the variation but is not the cause of the seasonal change itself.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — revolution of the earth on a tilted axis. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: the earth's rotation on its axis** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: the earth's revolution round the sun in an elliptical manner** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: latitudinal position of the place** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: revolution of the earth on a tilted axis** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

## Q15. The Narmada river flows to the west, while most other large peninsular rivers flow to the east. Why? 1...

**Primary classification:** Geography → India Drainage and River Interlinking → The Narmada river flows to the west, while most other large peninsular rivers flow to the east. Why? 1...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q15 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Geography/basic/05_Landforms-by-Running-Water.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Geography/basic/05_Landforms-by-Running-Water.md)
**Canonical slug:** `geography-the-narmada-river-flows-to-the-west-while-most-other-large-peninsular-rivers-flow-to-the-east-why-1-it-occupies-a-linear`

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The Narmada river flows to the west, while most other large peninsular rivers flow to the east. Why?
1. It occupies a linear rift valley.
2. It flows between the Vindhyas and the Satpuras.
3. The land slopes to the west from Central India.
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3  
(c) 1 and 3  
(d) None  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — 1 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Narmada river flows to the west primarily because it occupies a linear rift valley (Statement 1). This rift valley was formed due to faulting and subsidence during the upheaval of the Himalayas. While most of the Peninsular plateau slopes from west to east (making Statement 3 incorrect), the Narmada and Tapi rivers are exceptions because they flow through these structural troughs which are inclined westward (NCERT Class 11 Geography, Chapter 3). Statement 2 is a factually correct geographical description—the Narmada does flow between the Vindhya Range to the north and the Satpura Range to the south—but it is not the causal reason for the westward flow; a river could flow in either direction between two ranges. The specific westward direction is dictated by the rift valley's orientation and slope (UPSC Prelims 2013 Answer Key).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — 1 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

- **C: 1 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: None** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q16 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Geography/basic/04_Weathering-MassMovement-Groundwater.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Geography/basic/04_Weathering-MassMovement-Groundwater.md)
**Canonical slug:** `geography-on-the-planet-earth-most-of-the-freshwater-exists-as-ice-caps-and-glaciers-out-of-the-remaining-freshwater-the-largest-p`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

On the planet earth, most of the freshwater exists as ice caps and glaciers. Out of the remaining freshwater, the largest proportion

(a) is found in atmosphere as moisture and clouds  
(b) is found in freshwater lakes and rivers  
(c) exists as groundwater  
(d) exists as soil moisture  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — exists as groundwater**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

On Earth, approximately 97% of the total water is saline (found in oceans), while only about 3% is freshwater. According to the distribution of freshwater: 
1. **Ice caps and glaciers** account for the largest share, approximately **68.7% to 69%** of all freshwater.
2. **Groundwater** is the second-largest reservoir, accounting for approximately **30.1%** of the total freshwater.
3. **Surface water** (including lakes, rivers, and swamps) and other sources (such as soil moisture and atmospheric water vapor) make up the remaining **1.2%**.

Therefore, out of the 'remaining' freshwater (the portion not locked in ice caps and glaciers), groundwater represents the largest proportion. Specifically, freshwater lakes and rivers account for only about 0.3% of total freshwater, and atmospheric moisture accounts for even less (approx. 0.04%). This data is consistent across standard geographical references like NCERT and the USGS (United States Geological Survey).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — exists as groundwater. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: is found in atmosphere as moisture and clouds** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: is found in freshwater lakes and rivers** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: exists as groundwater** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: exists as soil moisture** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

## Q17. Consider the following pairs: 1. Nokrek Biosphere Reserve : Garo Hills 2. Logtak (Loktak) Lake : Barai...

**Primary classification:** Environment → Wetlands, Ramsar and Conservation Programmes → Consider the following pairs: 1. Nokrek Biosphere Reserve : Garo Hills 2. Logtak (Loktak) Lake : Barai...

**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/07_Biosphere-Reserves-and-Ramsar-Sites.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/07_Biosphere-Reserves-and-Ramsar-Sites.md)

**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following pairs:
1. Nokrek Biosphere Reserve : Garo Hills
2. Logtak (Loktak) Lake : Barail Range
3. Namdapha National Park : Dafla Hills
Which of the above pairs is/are correctly matched?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 1, 2 and 3  
(d) None  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — 1 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Based on geographical data and official records, only the first pair is correctly matched:

1. **Nokrek Biosphere Reserve : Garo Hills**: This pair is **correct**. The Nokrek Biosphere Reserve is located in the West Garo Hills district of Meghalaya. Nokrek Peak is the highest point in the Garo Hills range, reaching an altitude of 1,412 meters (UNESCO MAB; Meghalaya Tourism).

2. **Logtak (Loktak) Lake : Barail Range**: This pair is **incorrect**. Loktak Lake is the largest freshwater lake in Northeast India and is located in the central plains (Manipur Valley) of Manipur, specifically in the Bishnupur district. While the Barail Range is a significant mountain range in Assam, Manipur, and Nagaland, it is not the primary geographical feature associated with the lake's location; the lake is surrounded by the Manipur Hills (Manipur Tourism; Ramsar Sites Information Service).

3. **Namdapha National Park : Dafla Hills**: This pair is **incorrect**. Namdapha National Park is located in the Changlang district of Arunachal Pradesh, in the eastern extremity of the state. It is situated between the Dapha Bum range of the Mishmi Hills and the Patkai Range. The Dafla Hills are located much further west in Arunachal Pradesh, covering parts of West Kameng, East Kameng, and Papum Pare districts (UNESCO World Heritage Centre; Arunachal Pradesh Forest Department).

Therefore, only statement 1 is correct.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — 1 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

- **B: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: None** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

## Q18. Consider the following: 1. Electromagnetic radiation 2. Geothermal energy 3. Gravitational force 4. Pl...
**Primary route:** Geography → [The Earth and the Universe](../../topics/geography/the-earth-and-the-universe.md) → Consider the following: 1. Electromagnetic radiation 2. Geothermal energy 3. Gravitational force 4. Pl...
**Primary classification:** Geography → The Earth and the Universe → Consider the following: 1. Electromagnetic radiation 2. Geothermal energy 3. Gravitational force 4. Pl...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q18 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Geography/basic/01_The-Earth-and-the-Universe.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Geography/basic/01_The-Earth-and-the-Universe.md)
**Canonical slug:** `geography-consider-the-following-1-electromagnetic-radiation-2-geothermal-energy-3-gravitational-force-4-plate-movements-5-rotatio`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following:
1. Electromagnetic radiation
2. Geothermal energy
3. Gravitational force
4. Plate movements
5. Rotation of the earth
6. Revolution of the earth
Which of the above are responsible for bringing dynamic changes on the surface of the earth?

(a) 1, 2, 3 and 4 only  
(b) 1, 3, 5 and 6 only  
(c) 2, 4, 5 and 6 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

All the listed factors are responsible for bringing dynamic changes to the Earth's surface through endogenic (internal) and exogenic (external) processes. 1. Electromagnetic radiation (Solar Energy) is the primary driver of exogenic processes such as weathering, erosion, and the water cycle (NCERT Class 11, Ch 6). 2. Geothermal energy, originating from radioactive decay and primordial heat, drives endogenic processes like volcanism and plate tectonics (NCERT Class 11, Ch 6). 3. Gravitational force is a directional force that facilitates mass wasting, the flow of water/ice, and maintains the atmosphere (NCERT Class 11, Ch 6). 4. Plate movements (Tectonics) are responsible for large-scale surface changes including mountain building (orogeny) and the creation of oceanic basins (NCERT Class 11, Ch 4). 5. Rotation of the earth causes the Coriolis effect, which influences atmospheric circulation and ocean currents, thereby affecting erosion and deposition patterns; it also contributes to the Earth's equatorial bulge (NCERT Class 11, Ch 3 & 10). 6. Revolution of the earth, combined with the tilt of the axis, causes seasons, which dictate the intensity of geomorphic agents like frost, rain, and biological activity over time (NCERT Class 11, Ch 8).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1, 2, 3 and 4 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: 1, 3, 5 and 6 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: 2, 4, 5 and 6 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which of the following bodies does not/do not find mention in the Constitution?
1. National Development Council
2. Planning Commission
3. Zonal Councils
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 and 2 only  
(b) 2 only  
(c) 1 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — 1, 2 and 3**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

None of the three bodies mentioned—the National Development Council, the Planning Commission, and the Zonal Councils—are mentioned in the Constitution of India. The National Development Council (NDC) was established on August 6, 1952, by an executive resolution of the Government of India; it is neither a constitutional nor a statutory body (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter: Non-Constitutional Bodies). The Planning Commission was established on March 15, 1950, also by an executive resolution, and was neither a constitutional nor a statutory body (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter: Non-Constitutional Bodies). It has since been replaced by NITI Aayog, which is also an executive body. Zonal Councils were established under the States Reorganisation Act of 1956, making them statutory bodies rather than constitutional ones (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter: Interstate Relations). Therefore, all three are extra-constitutional bodies.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — 1, 2 and 3. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

- **C: 1 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

## Q20. The demand for the Tebhaga Peasant Movement in Bengal was for
**Primary route:** Modern History → [Left, Peasant, Workers and States’ Peoples’ Movements](../../topics/modern-history/left-peasant-workers-and-states-peoples-movements.md) → The demand for the Tebhaga Peasant Movement in Bengal was for
**Primary classification:** Modern History → Left, Peasant, Workers and States’ Peoples’ Movements → The demand for the Tebhaga Peasant Movement in Bengal was for
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q20 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Modern-Indian-History/basic/23_Left-Peasant-Workers-and-States-Peoples-Movements.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Modern-Indian-History/basic/23_Left-Peasant-Workers-and-States-Peoples-Movements.md)
**Canonical slug:** `modern-history-the-demand-for-the-tebhaga-peasant-movement-in-bengal-was-for`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The demand for the Tebhaga Peasant Movement in Bengal was for

(a) the reduction of the share of the landlords from one-half of the crop to one-third  
(b) the grant of ownership of land to peasants as they were the actual cultivators of the land  
(c) the uprooting of Zamindari system and the end of serfdom  
(d) writing off all peasant debts  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — the reduction of the share of the landlords from one-half of the crop to one-third**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Tebhaga movement (1946–1947) was a significant peasant agitation in Bengal led by the Bengal Provincial Kisan Sabha, the peasant wing of the Communist Party of India. The movement's primary demand was the implementation of the Floud Commission's (Land Revenue Commission) recommendation, which suggested that sharecroppers (known as bargadars) should retain two-thirds of the harvest for themselves, thereby reducing the landlord's (jotedar's) share from the traditional one-half (50%) to one-third (33%). The name 'Tebhaga' literally means 'three shares,' referring to this proposed division. The movement was characterized by the slogan 'Adhi noi, Tebhaga chai' (Not half, but three-shares). While broader peasant movements often aimed for the abolition of the Zamindari system, the specific and immediate focus of the Tebhaga movement was this crop-sharing ratio. (Sources: Bipan Chandra, 'India's Struggle for Independence', Chapter 36; Banglapedia - Tebhaga Movement; NCERT Class 12 History).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — the reduction of the share of the landlords from one-half of the crop to one-third. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: the reduction of the share of the landlords from one-half of the crop to one-third** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **B: the grant of ownership of land to peasants as they were the actual cultivators of the land** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: the uprooting of Zamindari system and the end of serfdom** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: writing off all peasant debts** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.
