<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q022"></a>
## Q22. In the grasslands, trees do not replace the grasses as a part of an ecological succession because of
**Primary route:** Environment → [Ecosystem Structure and Function](../../topics/environment/ecosystem-structure-and-function.md) → In the grasslands, trees do not replace the grasses as a part of an ecological succession because of
**Primary classification:** Environment → Ecosystem Structure and Function → In the grasslands, trees do not replace the grasses as a part of an ecological succession because of
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q22 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/03_Ecological-Succession-and-Biomes.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/03_Ecological-Succession-and-Biomes.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-in-the-grasslands-trees-do-not-replace-the-grasses-as-a-part-of-an-ecological-succession-because-of`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

In the grasslands, trees do not replace the grasses as a part of an ecological succession because of

(a) insects and fungi  
(b) limited sunlight and paucity of nutrients  
(c) water limits and fire  
(d) None of the above  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — water limits and fire**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

In grassland ecosystems, the transition or ecological succession from grasses to trees is primarily inhibited by 'water limits and fire'. Grasslands typically occur in regions where rainfall is sufficient to support grass but insufficient or too seasonal to support large-scale forest growth. During the dry season, the lack of moisture (water limits) makes it difficult for tree seedlings to survive. Furthermore, frequent fires—whether natural or man-made—play a crucial role in maintaining grasslands. Grasses have their growing points (meristems) at or below the soil surface, allowing them to regenerate quickly after a fire. In contrast, tree seedlings are often destroyed by fire before they can reach maturity. This combination of periodic drought and fire prevents woody plants from dominating the landscape (Source: NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 14; Shankar IAS Environment).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — water limits and fire. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: insects and fungi** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: limited sunlight and paucity of nutrients** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: water limits and fire** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: None of the above** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q023"></a>
## Q23. Which one of the following is the correct sequence of ecosystems in the order of decreasing productivity?
**Primary route:** Environment → [Ecosystem Structure and Function](../../topics/environment/ecosystem-structure-and-function.md) → Which one of the following is the correct sequence of ecosystems in the order of decreasing productivity?
**Primary classification:** Environment → Ecosystem Structure and Function → Which one of the following is the correct sequence of ecosystems in the order of decreasing productivity?
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q23 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01_Ecosystem-Structure-and-Function.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01_Ecosystem-Structure-and-Function.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-which-one-of-the-following-is-the-correct-sequence-of-ecosystems-in-the-order-of-decreasing-productivity`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which one of the following is the correct sequence of ecosystems in the order of decreasing productivity?

(a) Oceans, lakes, grasslands, mangroves  
(b) Mangroves, oceans, grasslands, lakes  
(c) Mangroves, grasslands, lakes, oceans  
(d) Oceans, mangroves, lakes, grasslands  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — Mangroves, grasslands, lakes, oceans**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The productivity of an ecosystem refers to the rate of biomass production per unit area per unit time, typically measured as Net Primary Productivity (NPP). The correct sequence in decreasing order of productivity is Mangroves, Grasslands, Lakes, and Oceans. 

1. **Mangroves (and Estuaries):** These are among the most productive ecosystems globally due to the high availability of nutrients from both land runoff and tidal mixing, combined with abundant sunlight. Their NPP is typically around 1500–2000 g/m²/yr (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 14).
2. **Grasslands:** These have moderate productivity. Tropical grasslands (savannas) have an NPP of about 900 g/m²/yr, while temperate grasslands are around 600 g/m²/yr (Shankar IAS Environment, Chapter 1).
3. **Lakes:** Freshwater ecosystems like lakes generally have lower productivity than terrestrial grasslands, with an average NPP of approximately 250–500 g/m²/yr, though this varies based on nutrient levels (oligotrophic vs. eutrophic).
4. **Oceans:** While the total global biomass production of oceans is high due to their vast area, their productivity *per unit area* is the lowest (approx. 125 g/m²/yr). Large parts of the open ocean are considered 'biological deserts' because they are nutrient-limited (PMF IAS; Whittaker, 1975).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — Mangroves, grasslands, lakes, oceans. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: Oceans, lakes, grasslands, mangroves** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: Mangroves, oceans, grasslands, lakes** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: Mangroves, grasslands, lakes, oceans** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: Oceans, mangroves, lakes, grasslands** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

## Q24. Contour bunding is a method of soil conservation used in
**Primary route:** Geography → [Aeolian Processes and Land Degradation](../../topics/geography/aeolian-processes-land-degradation.md) → Contour bunding is a method of soil conservation used in
**Primary classification:** Geography → Aeolian Processes and Land Degradation → Contour bunding is a method of soil conservation used in
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q24 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Geography/basic/07_Arid-Desert-Landforms.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Geography/basic/07_Arid-Desert-Landforms.md)
**Canonical slug:** `geography-contour-bunding-is-a-method-of-soil-conservation-used-in`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Contour bunding is a method of soil conservation used in

(a) desert margins, liable to strong wind action  
(b) low flat plains, close to stream courses, liable to flooding  
(c) scrublands, liable to spread of weed growth  
(d) None of the above  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — None of the above**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Contour bunding is a soil conservation technique used primarily on sloping or hilly terrains. It involves the construction of small embankments (bunds) along the contours of the land (lines of equal elevation). The primary purpose is to intercept surface runoff, reduce its velocity, and prevent soil erosion while promoting water infiltration into the ground (NCERT Class 10 Geography, Chapter 1). 

- Option 0 describes desert margins affected by wind action, where 'shelterbelts' or 'sand dune stabilization' are the standard conservation methods.
- Option 1 describes low flat plains liable to flooding, where 'embankments' or 'drainage management' are used, but 'contour' bunding is unnecessary due to the lack of slope.
- Option 2 describes scrublands and weed growth, which is managed through biological or mechanical weeding, not contour bunding.

Since contour bunding is specifically designed for undulating or sloping land to manage water-induced erosion, none of the specific scenarios in options 0, 1, or 2 are correct (NCERT Class 11 Geography, Chapter 6).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — None of the above. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: desert margins, liable to strong wind action** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: low flat plains, close to stream courses, liable to flooding** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: scrublands, liable to spread of weed growth** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: None of the above** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q25. The Government enacted the Panchayat Extension to Scheduled Areas (PESA) Act in 1996. Which one of the...

**Primary classification:** Polity → Scheduled and Tribal Areas → The Government enacted the Panchayat Extension to Scheduled Areas (PESA) Act in 1996. Which one of the...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q25 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Scheduled-and-Tribal-Areas.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Scheduled-and-Tribal-Areas.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-the-government-enacted-the-panchayat-extension-to-scheduled-areas-pesa-act-in-1996-which-one-of-the-following-is-not-ide`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The Government enacted the Panchayat Extension to Scheduled Areas (PESA) Act in 1996. Which one of the following is not identified as its objective?

(a) To provide self-governance  
(b) To recognize traditional rights  
(c) To create autonomous regions in tribal areas  
(d) To free tribal people from exploitation  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — To create autonomous regions in tribal areas**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Provisions of the Panchayats (Extension to the Scheduled Areas) Act, 1996 (PESA) was enacted to extend the Panchayati Raj system to the Fifth Schedule areas of India with specific modifications to protect tribal interests. Its primary objectives include: (0) providing self-governance or 'self-rule' to tribal populations by making the Gram Sabha the nucleus of all activities; (1) recognizing and preserving the traditional rights, customs, and religious practices of tribal communities; and (3) freeing tribal people from exploitation by giving Gram Sabhas power over land alienation, money lending, and minor forest produce. However, the objective 'to create autonomous regions in tribal areas' (Option 2) is associated with the Sixth Schedule of the Indian Constitution (Articles 244(2) and 275(1)), which provides for Autonomous District Councils and Regional Councils in the states of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram. PESA applies to Fifth Schedule areas and focuses on local self-governance through the Panchayat framework rather than creating separate autonomous administrative regions.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — To create autonomous regions in tribal areas. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: To provide self-governance** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: To recognize traditional rights** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: To create autonomous regions in tribal areas** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: To free tribal people from exploitation** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

## Q26. Under the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2...
**Primary route:** Environment → [Forest Types and Forest Rights Act](../../topics/environment/forest-types-and-forest-rights-act.md) → Under the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2...
**Primary classification:** Environment → Forest Types and Forest Rights Act → Under the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q26 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/11_Forest-Types-and-Forest-Rights-Act.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/11_Forest-Types-and-Forest-Rights-Act.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-under-the-scheduled-tribes-and-other-traditional-forest-dwellers-recognition-of-forest-rights-act-2006-who-shall-be-the`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Under the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2006, who shall be the authority to initiate the process for determining the nature and extent of individual or community forest rights or both?

(a) State Forest Department  
(b) District Collector/Deputy Commissioner  
(c) Tahsildar/Block Development Officer/Mandal Revenue Officer  
(d) Gram Sabha  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — Gram Sabha**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

According to Section 6(1) of the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2006 (commonly known as the Forest Rights Act or FRA), the Gram Sabha is the designated authority to initiate the process for determining the nature and extent of individual or community forest rights or both. The process involves the Gram Sabha receiving claims, consolidating and verifying them, and preparing a map delineating the area of each recommended claim. After this, the Gram Sabha passes a resolution and forwards it to the Sub-Divisional Level Committee (SDLC). The SDLC examines the resolution and prepares the record of forest rights, which is then forwarded to the District Level Committee (DLC) for a final decision and approval. While the Ministry of Tribal Affairs is the nodal agency at the central level, the grassroots initiation of the rights recognition process rests solely with the Gram Sabha to ensure a democratic and bottom-up approach to forest governance.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — Gram Sabha. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: State Forest Department** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: District Collector/Deputy Commissioner** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: Tahsildar/Block Development Officer/Mandal Revenue Officer** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: Gram Sabha** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q27. Improper handling and storage of cereal grains and oilseeds result in the production of toxins known a...
**Primary route:** Science and Technology → [General Science: Biology and Physiology](../../topics/science-and-technology/general-science-biology-and-physiology.md) → Improper handling and storage of cereal grains and oilseeds result in the production of toxins known a...
**Primary classification:** Science and Technology → General Science: Biology and Physiology → Improper handling and storage of cereal grains and oilseeds result in the production of toxins known a...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q27 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Science-and-Technology/basic/23_General-Science-Biology-and-Physiology.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Science-and-Technology/basic/23_General-Science-Biology-and-Physiology.md)
**Canonical slug:** `science-and-technology-improper-handling-and-storage-of-cereal-grains-and-oilseeds-result-in-the-production-of-toxins-known-as-aflatoxins-which`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Improper handling and storage of cereal grains and oilseeds result in the production of toxins known as aflatoxins which are not generally destroyed by normal cooking process. Aflatoxins are produced by

(a) bacteria  
(b) protozoa  
(c) moulds  
(d) viruses  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — moulds**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Aflatoxins are poisonous carcinogens and mutagens that are produced by certain moulds (fungi), specifically Aspergillus flavus and Aspergillus parasiticus. These moulds grow on soil, decaying vegetation, hay, and various grains, especially when stored under damp and warm conditions. Common crops affected include maize, peanuts, cottonseed, and tree nuts (WHO, 2018). Aflatoxins are chemically stable and possess high thermal stability; they are not destroyed by standard cooking temperatures (boiling, baking, or frying), making them a significant food safety concern (National Cancer Institute). Long-term exposure to aflatoxins is a known cause of liver cancer and can lead to aflatoxicosis in acute cases.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — moulds. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**

<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q028"></a>
## Q28. 'Economic Justice' as one of the objectives of the Indian Constitution has been provided in
**Primary route:** Polity → [Directive Principles](../../topics/polity/directive-principles.md) → 'Economic Justice' as one of the objectives of the Indian Constitution has been provided in
**Primary classification:** Polity → Directive Principles → 'Economic Justice' as one of the objectives of the Indian Constitution has been provided in
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q28 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Polity/basic/Directive-Principles.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Polity/basic/Directive-Principles.md)
**Canonical slug:** `polity-economic-justice-as-one-of-the-objectives-of-the-indian-constitution-has-been-provided-in`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

'Economic Justice' as one of the objectives of the Indian Constitution has been provided in

(a) the Preamble and the Fundamental Rights  
(b) the Preamble and the Directive Principles of State Policy  
(c) the Fundamental Rights and the Directive Principles of State Policy  
(d) None of the above  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — the Preamble and the Directive Principles of State Policy**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The objective of 'Economic Justice' is explicitly enshrined in the Indian Constitution within the Preamble and the Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP). The Preamble of the Constitution of India resolves to secure to all its citizens 'JUSTICE, social, economic and political' (M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 4). This vision is further detailed in Part IV of the Constitution under the Directive Principles. Specifically, Article 38(1) states that 'The State shall strive to promote the welfare of the people by securing and protecting as effectively as it may a social order in which justice, social, economic and political, shall inform all the institutions of the national life' (Constitution of India, Art. 38). While Fundamental Rights (Part III) focus on civil and political liberties to ensure political justice, the DPSP aims to establish social and economic democracy, making the Preamble and DPSP the primary locations for the objective of economic justice (NCERT Class 11, Indian Constitution at Work, Chapter 2).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — the Preamble and the Directive Principles of State Policy. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: the Preamble and the Fundamental Rights** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **B: the Preamble and the Directive Principles of State Policy** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **C: the Fundamental Rights and the Directive Principles of State Policy** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: None of the above** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option B. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

## Q29. Due to improper/indiscriminate disposal of old and used computers or their parts, which of the followi...

**Primary classification:** Environment → Solid, Plastic and E-Waste Rules → Due to improper/indiscriminate disposal of old and used computers or their parts, which of the followi...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q29 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/15_Solid-Plastic-and-E-Waste-Rules.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/15_Solid-Plastic-and-E-Waste-Rules.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-due-to-improper-indiscriminate-disposal-of-old-and-used-computers-or-their-parts-which-of-the-following-are-released-int`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Due to improper/indiscriminate disposal of old and used computers or their parts, which of the following are released into the environment as e-waste?
1. Beryllium
2. Cadmium
3. Chromium
4. Heptachlor
5. Mercury
6. Lead
7. Plutonium
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1, 3, 4, 6 and 7 only  
(b) 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6 only  
(c) 2, 4, 5 and 7 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 and 7  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The improper disposal of electronic waste (e-waste) from computers and their parts releases several hazardous heavy metals and toxic substances into the environment. 

1. **Beryllium (1)**: Used in motherboards, connectors, and power supply boxes due to its thermal conductivity and strength (Source: PMC NIH, ExamRobot).
2. **Cadmium (2)**: Found in rechargeable Ni-Cd batteries, computer speakers, and older CRT (Cathode Ray Tube) monitors (Source: NextIAS, Drishti IAS).
3. **Chromium (3)**: Specifically hexavalent chromium is used as a corrosion-resistant coating for metal housings and in printed circuit boards (Source: SuperKalam, Prepp).
4. **Mercury (5)**: Used in LCD monitor backlighting (CCFLs), switches, and some printed circuit boards (Source: E-Waste Management Rules, Beyond Surplus).
5. **Lead (6)**: A major component in solder on circuit boards and in the glass of CRT monitors (Source: PIB, NCERT Class 12 Chemistry).

In contrast, **Heptachlor (4)** is an organochlorine pesticide used for termite and insect control and is not a component of computer hardware (Source: CDC ATSDR). **Plutonium (7)** is a radioactive element used in nuclear reactors and weapons and is never found in consumer electronics or computers (Source: canonical topic evidence, WIPP). Therefore, the correct combination is 1, 2, 3, 5, and 6.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1, 3, 4, 6 and 7 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **B: 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **C: 2, 4, 5 and 7 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 and 7** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option B. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q30. Acid rain is caused by the pollution of environment by
**Primary route:** Environment → [Air Pollution and CPCB Standards](../../topics/environment/air-pollution-and-cpcb-standards.md) → Acid rain is caused by the pollution of environment by
**Primary classification:** Environment → Air Pollution and CPCB Standards → Acid rain is caused by the pollution of environment by

**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/13_Air-Pollution-and-CPCB-Standards.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/13_Air-Pollution-and-CPCB-Standards.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-acid-rain-is-caused-by-the-pollution-of-environment-by`

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Acid rain is caused by the pollution of environment by

(a) carbon dioxide and nitrogen  
(b) carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide  
(c) ozone and carbon dioxide  
(d) nitrous oxide and sulphur dioxide  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — nitrous oxide and sulphur dioxide**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Acid rain is primarily caused by the emission of sulfur dioxide (SO2) and nitrogen oxides (NOx) into the atmosphere. These pollutants react with water vapor, oxygen, and other chemicals in the air to form sulfuric acid and nitric acid, which then fall to the ground as wet or dry deposition. According to NCERT Class 11 Chemistry (Chapter 14: Environmental Chemistry), the burning of fossil fuels containing sulfur and nitrogen leads to the production of SO2 and NO2. While 'nitrous oxide' (N2O) is technically a specific oxide of nitrogen, it is used in this UPSC question context to represent the broader category of nitrogen oxides that contribute to atmospheric acidity. The official UPSC 2013 answer key confirms this option as correct.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — nitrous oxide and sulphur dioxide. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: carbon dioxide and nitrogen** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: ozone and carbon dioxide** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: nitrous oxide and sulphur dioxide** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q31. With reference to food chains in ecosystems, consider the following statements: 1. A food chain illust...

**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01_Ecosystem-Structure-and-Function.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/01_Ecosystem-Structure-and-Function.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-with-reference-to-food-chains-in-ecosystems-consider-the-following-statements-1-a-food-chain-illustrates-the-order-in-wh`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

With reference to food chains in ecosystems, consider the following statements:
1. A food chain illustrates the order in which a chain of organisms feed upon each other.
2. Food chains are found within the populations of a species.
3. A food chain illustrates the numbers of each organism which are eaten by others.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?

(a) 1 only  
(b) 1 and 2 only  
(c) 1, 2 and 3  
(d) None  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — 1 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Statement 1 is correct: A food chain is a linear sequence of organisms through which nutrients and energy pass as one organism eats another. It illustrates the specific order of 'who eats whom' in an ecosystem (NCERT Class 10 Science, Chapter 15). Statement 2 is incorrect: Food chains describe the feeding relationships between different species at different trophic levels (e.g., producers, primary consumers, secondary consumers). They do not occur 'within the populations of a species,' as a population consists of individuals of the same species (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 13). Statement 3 is incorrect: A food chain illustrates the flow of energy and the sequence of consumption, but it does not quantify the number of organisms at each level. The graphical representation of the number of individuals at each trophic level is known as the 'Pyramid of Numbers,' which is a specific type of ecological pyramid (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 14). Therefore, only statement 1 is correct.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — 1 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **B: 1 and 2 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: None** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following pairs:

| National Park | River flowing through the Park |
|---|---|
| 1. Corbett National Park | Ganga |
| 2. Kaziranga National Park | Manas |
| 3. Silent Valley National Park | Kaveri |

Which of the above pairs is/are correctly matched?

(a) 1 and 2  
(b) 3 only  
(c) 1 and 3  
(d) None  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — None**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

None of the pairs are correctly matched based on geographical facts: 
1. **Corbett National Park**: The primary river flowing through this park is the **Ramganga**, a tributary of the Ganga. While the Ramganga eventually joins the Ganga, the Ganga itself does not flow through the park boundaries (Source: Jim Corbett National Park Official Website; NCERT Class 11 Geography: India Physical Environment). 
2. **Kaziranga National Park**: This park is situated on the banks of the **Brahmaputra River**. Other rivers like Diphlu and Mora Dhansiri also flow through it. The **Manas River** flows through the Manas National Park, which is located further west in Assam (Source: Kaziranga National Park Official Website; UNESCO World Heritage Centre). 
3. **Silent Valley National Park**: The river that flows through this park is the **Kunthipuzha**, which is a tributary of the Bharathapuzha river. The Kaveri river flows through different regions of Karnataka and Tamil Nadu and does not pass through Silent Valley in Kerala (Source: Kerala Forest Department - Silent Valley National Park; NCERT Class 11 Geography).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — None. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

## Q33. Consider the following organisms: 1. Agaricus 2. Nostoc 3. Spirogyra Which of the above is/are used as...

**Primary classification:** Science and Technology → General Science: Biology and Physiology → Consider the following organisms: 1. Agaricus 2. Nostoc 3. Spirogyra Which of the above is/are used as...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q33 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Science-and-Technology/basic/23_General-Science-Biology-and-Physiology.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Science-and-Technology/basic/23_General-Science-Biology-and-Physiology.md)
**Canonical slug:** `science-and-technology-consider-the-following-organisms-1-agaricus-2-nostoc-3-spirogyra-which-of-the-above-is-are-used-as-biofertilizer-biofert`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Consider the following organisms:
1. Agaricus
2. Nostoc
3. Spirogyra
Which of the above is/are used as biofertilizer/biofertilizers?

(a) 1 and 2  
(b) 2 only  
(c) 2 and 3  
(d) 3 only  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — 2 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

Among the given organisms, only Nostoc is used as a biofertilizer. 

1. **Nostoc**: It is a genus of cyanobacteria (blue-green algae) that can fix atmospheric nitrogen into a form (ammonia) that plants can readily absorb. This process occurs in specialized cells called heterocysts. Nostoc is widely used as a biofertilizer, particularly in rice (paddy) cultivation, to enhance soil fertility naturally (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 10; NCERT Class 11 Biology, Chapter 2).

2. **Agaricus**: This is a genus of mushrooms (fungi), which includes the common edible button mushroom. Agaricus species are saprophytic decomposers that break down organic matter. While certain fungi like Mycorrhizae (e.g., Glomus) act as biofertilizers by helping plants absorb phosphorus, Agaricus itself is not used as a biofertilizer (NCERT Class 11 Biology, Chapter 2).

3. **Spirogyra**: This is a filamentous green alga commonly found in freshwater. Although it is photosynthetic and contributes to oxygen levels in aquatic ecosystems, it does not fix atmospheric nitrogen and is not classified or used as a biofertilizer (NCERT Class 11 Biology, Chapter 3).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — 2 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

## Q34. Which of the following adds/add nitrogen to the soil? 1. Excretion of urea by animals 2. Burning of co...

**Primary classification:** Environment → Biogeochemical Cycles and Ecological Pyramids → Which of the following adds/add nitrogen to the soil? 1. Excretion of urea by animals 2. Burning of co...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q34 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination

**Canonical slug:** `environment-which-of-the-following-adds-add-nitrogen-to-the-soil-1-excretion-of-urea-by-animals-2-burning-of-coal-by-man-3-death-of`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which of the following adds/add nitrogen to the soil?
1. Excretion of urea by animals
2. Burning of coal by man
3. Death of vegetation
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 only  
(b) 2 and 3 only  
(c) 1 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2 and 3  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — 1 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The nitrogen cycle describes how nitrogen moves between the atmosphere, biosphere, and geosphere. 1. **Excretion of urea by animals**: Animals excrete nitrogenous waste products such as urea, uric acid, and ammonia. In the soil, these compounds are broken down by urease-producing bacteria into ammonia, which is then converted into nitrites and nitrates by nitrifying bacteria (Nitrosomonas and Nitrobacter), thereby adding nitrogen to the soil (NCERT Class 9 Science, Chapter 14). 2. **Burning of coal**: Burning coal releases nitrogen oxides (NOx) and other gases into the atmosphere. While a negligible amount might return to the soil through acid rain, the primary effect is atmospheric pollution rather than soil enrichment. In the context of the nitrogen cycle, it is not considered a process that 'adds nitrogen to the soil' in the same way biological processes do. 3. **Death of vegetation**: When plants die, the organic nitrogen stored in their tissues (proteins, nucleic acids) is decomposed by fungi and bacteria in a process called ammonification. This converts organic nitrogen into inorganic ammonium, which then enters the nitrification process to become available in the soil (NCERT Class 12 Biology, Chapter 14). Therefore, statements 1 and 3 are correct.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — 1 and 3 only. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: 1 and 3 only** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: 1, 2 and 3** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. ## Q35. In which of the following States is lion-tailed macaque found in its natural habitat? 1. Tamil Nadu 2....
**Primary route:** Environment → [Species and Current-Affairs Tracker](../../topics/environment/species-and-current-affairs-tracker.md) → In which of the following States is lion-tailed macaque found in its natural habitat? 1. Tamil Nadu 2....

**Canonical owner:** [Environment-and-Ecology/basic/28_Species-and-Current-Affairs-Tracker.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Environment-and-Ecology/basic/28_Species-and-Current-Affairs-Tracker.md)
**Canonical slug:** `environment-in-which-of-the-following-states-is-lion-tailed-macaque-found-in-its-natural-habitat-1-tamil-nadu-2-kerala-3-karnataka-4`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

In which of the following States is lion-tailed macaque found in its natural habitat?
1. Tamil Nadu
2. Kerala
3. Karnataka
4. Andhra Pradesh
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1, 2 and 3 only  
(b) 2 only  
(c) 1, 3 and 4 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — 1, 2 and 3 only**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The lion-tailed macaque (Macaca silenus) is an endangered primate endemic to the Western Ghats of South India. Its natural habitat is strictly confined to the tropical moist evergreen forests and semi-evergreen forests of the Western Ghats, which are spread across the states of Karnataka, Kerala, and Tamil Nadu (Drishti IAS, 2024; BYJU'S). It is not found in Andhra Pradesh, as the species is restricted to the high-rainfall regions of the Western Ghats, whereas Andhra Pradesh is primarily situated along the Eastern Ghats and the Deccan Plateau (Shankar IAS). Key protected areas where they are found include Silent Valley National Park and Periyar National Park in Kerala, Kudremukh National Park and Someshwara Wildlife Sanctuary in Karnataka, and the Anamalai Tiger Reserve and Kalakad-Mundanthurai Tiger Reserve in Tamil Nadu (canonical topic evidence; Victor Growth). The species is listed as 'Endangered' on the IUCN Red List and is protected under Schedule I of the Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972.

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Some Buddhist rock-cut caves are called Chaityas, while the others are called Viharas. What is the difference between the two?

(a) Vihara is a place of worship, while Chaitya is the dwelling place of the monks  
(b) Chaitya is a place of worship, while Vihara is the dwelling place of the monks  
(c) Chaitya is the stupa at the far end of the cave, while Vihara is the hall axial to it  
(d) There is no material difference between the two  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — Chaitya is a place of worship, while Vihara is the dwelling place of the monks**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

In Buddhist architecture, the primary distinction between a Chaitya and a Vihara lies in their functional purpose. A Chaitya (derived from the Sanskrit word 'Chita' meaning a funeral pyre) is a prayer hall or a shrine that typically houses a stupa at one end, serving as a place of congregational worship (NCERT Class 11, An Introduction to Indian Art, Chapter 4). In contrast, a Vihara is a monastery or a residential hall designed as a dwelling place for monks (Bhikshus). Viharas usually consist of a central courtyard or hall surrounded by small individual cells where monks lived and meditated (CCRT India, Buddhist Architecture). While early Viharas were simple residential units, later ones (like those at Ajanta) often incorporated shrines, but their primary historical definition remains a place of residence.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — Chaitya is a place of worship, while Vihara is the dwelling place of the monks. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

- **C: Chaitya is the stupa at the far end of the cave, while Vihara is the hall axial to it** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: There is no material difference between the two** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. ## Q37. Which one of the following describes best the concept of Nirvana in Buddhism?
**Primary route:** Ancient History → [Jainism and Buddhism](../../topics/ancient-history/jainism-and-buddhism.md) → Which one of the following describes best the concept of Nirvana in Buddhism?
**Primary classification:** Ancient History → Jainism and Buddhism → Which one of the following describes best the concept of Nirvana in Buddhism?
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q37 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Ancient-Indian-History/basic/10_Jainism-and-Buddhism.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Ancient-Indian-History/basic/10_Jainism-and-Buddhism.md)
**Canonical slug:** `ancient-history-which-one-of-the-following-describes-best-the-concept-of-nirvana-in-buddhism`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Which one of the following describes best the concept of Nirvana in Buddhism?

(a) The extinction of the flame of desire  
(b) The complete annihilation of self  
(c) A state of bliss and rest  
(d) A mental stage beyond all comprehension  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — The extinction of the flame of desire**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The term 'Nirvana' (or 'Nibbana' in Pali) literally translates to 'blowing out' or 'extinguishing,' much like a candle flame being put out. In Buddhist philosophy, it signifies the extinction of the 'three fires' or 'three poisons': greed (raga), hatred (dvesha), and delusion (moha). According to the Four Noble Truths, the root cause of suffering (Dukkha) is desire or craving (Tanha). Therefore, the cessation of suffering is achieved through the extinction of the flame of desire, which leads to liberation from the cycle of rebirth (Samsara). While Nirvana is often associated with peace and bliss, the most precise conceptual definition in early Buddhist doctrine is the cessation of the fires of desire and ignorance. Buddhism specifically rejects the 'complete annihilation of self' (Ucchedavada), as it teaches the doctrine of 'Anatta' (non-self), suggesting that there is no permanent self to be annihilated in the first place. Instead, Nirvana is the end of the ego-driven process of craving (NCERT Class 12 History, Chapter 4; Britannica).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — The extinction of the flame of desire. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: The extinction of the flame of desire** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **B: The complete annihilation of self** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: A state of bliss and rest** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: A mental stage beyond all comprehension** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

According to the Constitution of India, which of the following are fundamental for the governance of the country?

(a) Fundamental Rights  
(b) Fundamental Duties  
(c) Directive Principles of State Policy  
(d) Fundamental Rights and Fundamental Duties  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — Directive Principles of State Policy**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

According to Article 37 of the Constitution of India, the Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP) are explicitly described as 'fundamental in the governance of the country'. Article 37 states: 'The provisions contained in this Part [Part IV] shall not be enforceable by any court, but the principles therein laid down are nevertheless fundamental in the governance of the country and it shall be the duty of the State to apply these principles in making laws.' While Fundamental Rights (Part III) are justiciable and enforceable by courts, and Fundamental Duties (Part IV-A) are moral obligations of citizens, the DPSPs serve as the guiding principles for the State to ensure social and economic justice through legislation and policy-making (Constitution of India, Art. 37; M. Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 8).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — Directive Principles of State Policy. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

- **C: Directive Principles of State Policy** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q039"></a>
## Q39. The people of India agitated against the arrival of Simon Commission because
**Primary route:** Modern History → [Simon Commission, Nehru Report, CDM and RTC](../../topics/modern-history/simon-nehru-report-cdm-and-rtc.md) → The people of India agitated against the arrival of Simon Commission because
**Primary classification:** Modern History → Simon Commission, Nehru Report, CDM and RTC → The people of India agitated against the arrival of Simon Commission because
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q39 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Modern-Indian-History/basic/22_Simon-Nehru-Report-CDM-and-RTC.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Modern-Indian-History/basic/22_Simon-Nehru-Report-CDM-and-RTC.md)
**Canonical slug:** `modern-history-the-people-of-india-agitated-against-the-arrival-of-simon-commission-because`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The people of India agitated against the arrival of Simon Commission because

(a) Indians never wanted the review of the working of the Act of 1919  
(b) Simon Commission recommended the abolition of Dyarchy (Diarchy) in the Provinces  
(c) there was no Indian member in the Simon Commission  
(d) the Simon Commission suggested the partition of the country  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (C) — there was no Indian member in the Simon Commission**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Simon Commission, officially known as the Indian Statutory Commission, was appointed by the British Government in November 1927 (two years ahead of schedule) to report on the working of the Government of India Act of 1919 and suggest further constitutional reforms. The primary reason for the intense agitation and the famous slogan 'Simon Go Back' was that the commission was composed entirely of seven British members of Parliament, with no Indian representation. Indians viewed this 'all-white' composition as a violation of the principle of self-determination and a deliberate insult to their ability to decide their own constitutional future (NCERT Class 10 History, Chapter 2; Laxmikanth, Indian Polity, Chapter 1). While the commission eventually recommended the abolition of Dyarchy in its 1930 report, the protests were triggered by its arrival and composition, not its eventual findings. All major Indian political parties, including the Indian National Congress and the Muslim League, boycotted the commission (Bipin Chandra, India's Struggle for Independence).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (C) — there was no Indian member in the Simon Commission. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: Indians never wanted the review of the working of the Act of 1919** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **B: Simon Commission recommended the abolition of Dyarchy (Diarchy) in the Provinces** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.
- **C: there was no Indian member in the Simon Commission** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **D: the Simon Commission suggested the partition of the country** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option C.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option C. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

<a id="2013-prelims-gs1-q040"></a>
## Q40. Quit India Movement was launched in response to
**Primary route:** Modern History → [World War II, Cripps Mission and Quit India](../../topics/modern-history/wwii-cripps-mission-and-quit-india.md) → Quit India Movement was launched in response to
**Primary classification:** Modern History → World War II, Cripps Mission and Quit India → Quit India Movement was launched in response to
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q40 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Modern-Indian-History/basic/25_WWII-Cripps-Mission-and-Quit-India.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Modern-Indian-History/basic/25_WWII-Cripps-Mission-and-Quit-India.md)
**Canonical slug:** `modern-history-quit-india-movement-was-launched-in-response-to`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

Quit India Movement was launched in response to

(a) Cabinet Mission Plan  
(b) Cripps Proposals  
(c) Simon Commission Report  
(d) Wavell Plan  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (B) — Cripps Proposals**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Quit India Movement (Bharat Chhodo Andolan) was launched by Mahatma Gandhi on August 8, 1942, at the Gowalia Tank Maidan in Bombay. The primary trigger for the movement was the failure of the Cripps Mission in March 1942. Sir Stafford Cripps was sent to India to negotiate for Indian support in World War II, but his proposals—which offered 'Dominion Status' only after the war and allowed provinces the right to secede—were rejected by both the Indian National Congress and the Muslim League. Mahatma Gandhi famously described the Cripps offer as a 'post-dated cheque on a crashing bank.' The failure of these talks convinced the Indian leadership that the British had no real intention of granting immediate independence, leading to the 'Do or Die' call. Other options are chronologically incorrect: the Simon Commission arrived in 1928, the Wavell Plan was proposed in 1945, and the Cabinet Mission Plan was introduced in 1946 (NCERT Class 12 History, Chapter 13; PIB Release ID: 150118).

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (B) — Cripps Proposals. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: Cabinet Mission Plan** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **B: Cripps Proposals** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **C: Simon Commission Report** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.
- **D: Wavell Plan** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option B.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option B. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.

**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The balance of payments of a country is a systematic record of

(a) all import and export transactions of a country during a given period of time, normally a year  
(b) goods exported from a country during a year  
(c) economic transaction between the government of one country to another  
(d) capital movements from one country to another  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (A) — all import and export transactions of a country during a given period of time, normally a year**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Balance of Payments (BoP) is defined as a systematic record of all economic transactions between the residents of a country and the rest of the world during a specific period, typically a year. According to NCERT Class 12 Macroeconomics (Chapter 6), these transactions are categorized into the Current Account and the Capital Account. The Current Account includes the export and import of goods (merchandise), services (invisibles), and transfer payments. The Capital Account records all international transactions of assets such as money, stocks, bonds, and real estate. Option 0 is the most accurate among the choices as it encompasses the broad scope of inflows and outflows (imports and exports of value) that constitute the BoP. Option 1 refers only to the Balance of Trade (goods). Option 2 is incorrect because BoP includes transactions by individuals and businesses, not just governments. Option 3 refers only to the Capital Account component of the BoP.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (A) — all import and export transactions of a country during a given period of time, normally a year. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: all import and export transactions of a country during a given period of time, normally a year** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.
- **B: goods exported from a country during a year** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **C: economic transaction between the government of one country to another** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.
- **D: capital movements from one country to another** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option A.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option A. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

**Primary classification:** Economy → Banking Structure, NBFCs and Financial Regulation → The Reserve Bank of India regulates the commercial banks in matters of 1. liquidity of assets 2. branc...
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q42 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Economy/basic/05_Banking-Structure-NBFCs-and-Financial-Regulation.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Economy/basic/05_Banking-Structure-NBFCs-and-Financial-Regulation.md)
**Canonical slug:** `economy-the-reserve-bank-of-india-regulates-the-commercial-banks-in-matters-of-1-liquidity-of-assets-2-branch-expansion-3-merger`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

The Reserve Bank of India regulates the commercial banks in matters of
1. liquidity of assets
2. branch expansion
3. merger of banks
4. winding-up of banks
Select the correct answer using the codes given below.

(a) 1 and 4 only  
(b) 2, 3 and 4 only  
(c) 1, 2 and 3 only  
(d) 1, 2, 3 and 4  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — 1, 2, 3 and 4**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) exercises comprehensive regulatory and supervisory powers over commercial banks in India, primarily derived from the Banking Regulation Act, 1949. 
1. **Liquidity of assets**: Under Section 24 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949, the RBI regulates the liquidity of banks through the Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR), which requires banks to maintain a specific percentage of their Net Demand and Time Liabilities (NDTL) in liquid assets like gold, cash, or approved securities.
2. **Branch expansion**: Section 23 of the Act mandates that banking companies must obtain prior permission from the RBI to open new branches or change the location of existing ones, ensuring a balanced spread of banking services.
3. **Merger of banks**: The RBI has the authority to oversee both voluntary and compulsory mergers (amalgamations) of banks under Sections 44A and 45 of the Act to protect depositors' interests and maintain financial stability.
4. **Winding-up of banks**: Under Section 38, the RBI can apply to the High Court for the winding up of a banking company if it is unable to pay its debts or if its continued existence is prejudicial to the interests of the depositors. 
Therefore, all four statements are correct.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — 1, 2, 3 and 4. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: 1 and 4 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: 2, 3 and 4 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: 1, 2 and 3 only** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: 1, 2, 3 and 4** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.

## Q43. An increase in the Bank Rate generally indicates that the
**Primary route:** Economy → [RBI, Monetary Policy and Liquidity Management](../../topics/economy/rbi-monetary-policy-and-liquidity-management.md) → An increase in the Bank Rate generally indicates that the
**Primary classification:** Economy → RBI, Monetary Policy and Liquidity Management → An increase in the Bank Rate generally indicates that the
**Metadata:** 2013 · Prelims · GS-I · Series A · Official Q43 · Difficulty: Moderate · Demand: factual/conceptual statement or option evaluation · elimination
**Canonical owner:** [Economy/basic/04_RBI-Monetary-Policy-and-Liquidity-Management.md](../../../upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Economy/basic/04_RBI-Monetary-Policy-and-Liquidity-Management.md)
**Canonical slug:** `economy-an-increase-in-the-bank-rate-generally-indicates-that-the`
**Backlink:** [Paper-wise index](../../catalogue/PAPERWISE-INDEX.md)

### Official Series-A question (scan-reconciled transcription)

An increase in the Bank Rate generally indicates that the

(a) market rate of interest is likely to fall  
(b) Central Bank is no longer making loans to commercial banks  
(c) Central Bank is following an easy money policy  
(d) Central Bank is following a tight money policy  

**Answer (UPSC official-final Series-A key): (D) — Central Bank is following a tight money policy**

### Statement/option evaluation, elimination logic and trap

An increase in the Bank Rate generally indicates that the Central Bank is following a tight money policy (also known as a contractionary monetary policy). The Bank Rate is the standard rate at which the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) is prepared to buy or re-discount bills of exchange or other commercial papers eligible for purchase under the RBI Act, 1934 (Section 49). In practice, it is the rate at which the central bank lends long-term funds to commercial banks without any collateral (NCERT Class 12 Macroeconomics, Chapter 3). When the RBI increases the Bank Rate, the cost of borrowing for commercial banks rises. To maintain their profit margins, commercial banks subsequently increase the interest rates they charge their customers for loans. This rise in market interest rates discourages borrowing and spending by consumers and businesses, leading to a reduction in the overall money supply in the economy. This mechanism is primarily used to control inflation and stabilize the currency (RBI Monetary Policy Tools; Vajiram & Ravi). Conversely, an 'easy money policy' involves lowering the Bank Rate to stimulate economic growth by making credit cheaper and increasing the money supply.

**Statement/item-level result:**
- **Official keyed answer:** (D) — Central Bank is following a tight money policy. The numbered statements, pairs or alternatives resolve under the controlling explanation above; each competing code either includes a false item, excludes a true item, or asserts the wrong relationship.

**Option-by-option check:**
- **A: market rate of interest is likely to fall** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **B: Central Bank is no longer making loans to commercial banks** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **C: Central Bank is following an easy money policy** — Incorrect: it conflicts with the controlling facts, includes an incorrect proposition, omits a required proposition, or states the wrong association relative to option D.
- **D: Central Bank is following a tight money policy** — Correct: it matches the Commission final Series-A key and the controlling facts explained above.

**Elimination logic:** Test every numbered statement or factual association independently, beginning with the decisive fact in the explanation. Retain only the code consistent with all verified items; here that yields option D. Elimination corroborates knowledge and is not treated as proof by itself.
**UPSC trap:** Watch for absolute qualifiers, reversed cause/effect, adjacent but wrong institutions or locations, and code choices made attractive by one true statement despite another false statement.
